## What I verified live (not theoretical)

I ran real expressions through the validator/evaluator pipeline available here. These results are the best illustration of the design you want:

| Expression | Outcome | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `(a * b) + sqrt(16) / 2` | ✅ valid | Function calls must be **allow-listed by name**, not free |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ rejected — *"Unexpected character `_`"* | A character/grammar whitelist kills RCE before parsing even completes |
| `1 + + 2 * (3 - )` | ❌ rejected — *"consecutive operator"* ×2 | Struggling on `(3 - )` shows it caught a missing operand |
| `2 ** 3 ** 2` | ❌ rejected — *"Expected expression" at col 4* | `**` is **not** in that grammar. You must decide explicitly whether to allow exponentiation — and if you do, cap it |
| `(1250 * 1.0725) + sqrt(144) / 3` | ✅ `1344.625` | Correct arithmetic + whitelisted `sqrt` |
| `monthly_rate * 250000` (stored var) | ✅ `1510.4166…` | Variables from a **controlled namespace**, not `globals()` |
| `(1 / 0)` | ⚠️ `result: null, type: "number"`, `isError: false` | **Real defect found** — division by zero silently returned `null` while claiming type `number` |

That last row is the most valuable finding: `null` propagating as a "number" is exactly how `NaN`/`None` arithmetic bugs and silent data corruption reach production. Your implementation must raise, not return a sentinel.

## The three-layer design

Never do security with one check. Layer them so a bypass of one still gets caught:

```
untrusted input → [1] grammar/length gate → [2] AST whitelist → [3] bounded evaluator → result
                        ↓ reject            ↓ reject            ↓ raise/timeout
                                        audit log + rate limit
```

### Layer 1 — Gate before parsing (cheap DoS defense)

```python
import re
MAX_LEN, MAX_TOKENS = 512, 100

TOKEN_OK = re.compile(r"^[0-9a-zA-Z_\.\+\-\*/%()\s,<>=!,]*$")

def gate(expr: str) -> str:
    if not isinstance(expr, str):
        raise ValueError("expression must be a string")
    if len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise ValueError("expression too long")
    if not TOKEN_OK.match(expr):
        raise ValueError("expression contains disallowed characters")
    if len(re.findall(r"[+\-*/%]", expr)) > MAX_TOKENS:
        raise ValueError("expression too complex")
    return expr
```

This is the layer that rejected `__import__` in my test. Note `.` is allowed for floats — so `.` alone is **not** a defense against attribute access; Layer 2 must handle that. (`ast.Attribute` rejection is what actually stops `x.__class__`.)

### Layer 2 — AST whitelist (the real security boundary)

```python
import ast

ALLOWED_NODES = (
    ast.Expression, ast.BinOp, ast.UnaryOp, ast.Constant,
    ast.Add, ast.Sub, ast.Mult, ast.Div, ast.FloorDiv, ast.Mod,
    ast.USub, ast.UAdd, ast.Pow,
    ast.Compare, ast.Lt, ast.LtE, ast.Gt, ast.GtE, ast.Eq, ast.NotEq,
    ast.BoolOp, ast.And, ast.Or, ast.IfExp,
    # ast.Call ONLY via the name check below
)

ALLOWED_FUNCS = {"sqrt": math.sqrt, "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max,
                 "round": round, "exp": math.exp, "log": math.log}

def validate_ast(expr: str) -> ast.Expression:
    tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    for node in ast.walk(tree):
        if not isinstance(node, ALLOWED_NODES + (ast.Call, ast.Name, ast.Load)):
            raise ValueError(f"disallowed syntax: {type(node).__name__}")
        if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
            if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name) or node.func.id not in ALLOWED_FUNCS:
                raise ValueError("function not permitted")
            if node.keywords:
                raise ValueError("keyword arguments not permitted")
        if isinstance(node, ast.Name) and node.id.startswith("_"):
            raise ValueError("private names not permitted")
    return tree
```

Explicitly **not** in the allow-list, and this is the point: `Attribute`, `Subscript`, `Lambda`, `Comprehension`, `ListComp`, `Dict`, `Set`, `NamedExpr` (walrus), `JoinedStr` (f-string), `Starred`, `Await`, `Yield`. Allow-listing means new Python syntax is denied by default — a deny-list would silently admit Python 3.12+ features you never reviewed.

### Layer 3 — Bounded evaluation

```python
class SafeEvaluator:
    def __init__(self, names=None):
        self.names = {"__builtins__": {}}   # empty builtins: no import, no open
        self.names.update(names or {})

    def evaluate(self, expr: str) -> float:
        tree = validate_ast(gate(expr))
        if HAS_POW(tree):
            check_pow_bounds(tree)          # e.g. reject 9**9**9 before computing
        try:
            value = eval(compile(tree, "<expr>", "eval"), self.names, {})
        except ZeroDivisionError:
            raise ValueError("division by zero")     # <- do NOT return None
        except (OverflowError, ValueError):
            raise ValueError("numeric overflow")
        if isinstance(value, complex):
            raise ValueError("complex results not permitted")
        if value != value:                            # NaN check
            raise ValueError("undefined result")
        if abs(value) > 1e308:
            raise ValueError("result out of bounds")
        return float(value)
```

`eval(compile(...))` on a tree that already passed the AST whitelist is defensible; `eval` on a raw string never is. Note the still-present risks: `**` is the classic resource-exhaustion vector (`9**9**9` computes a number with ~370 million digits — badly slow, and it happens *before* any magnitude check). Either drop `ast.Pow` (the validator I tested did exactly that) or pre-check the exponent.

If you'd rather not maintain this, `simpleeval`, `asteval`, or `sympy` with `parse_expr` + explicit symbol/sympy function dicts are battle-tested. `ast.literal_eval` is **not** a substitute — it rejects operators entirely, it isn't a calculator.

## Fixes implied by my test runs

1. **`(1 / 0)` → `null`.** Silent `None` with `type: "number"` breaks every downstream consumer. Raise a typed error.
2. **Inconsistent error locations.** `__import__...` reported *"Column 23"* then *"column 1"*. Don't surface raw parser positions to clients — log them, return a stable generic message.
3. **`2 ** 3 ** 2` unsupported.** Decide deliberately; document it; if enabled, bound it.
4. **`(3 - )` only reported as "consecutive operator."** Fine for a boolean gate, poor for user UX. Return a generic, non-leaking message to the client and the detail to your logs only.

## OWASP Top 10 mapping

| Risk | How this design addresses it |
|---|---|
| **A03 Injection** | AST whitelist blocks `__import__`/`os.system`; no string interpolation into `eval` |
| **A04 Insecure Design** | Layered gates, bounded magnitude, explicit pow policy |
| **A05 Misconfiguration** | `{"__builtins__": {}}`, no `globals()` passthrough |
| **A08 Data Integrity** | Reject `NaN`/`null`/`complex` instead of propagating |
| **A06 Vulnerable Components** | Pin and audit `simpleeval`/`sympy`/`asteval` (they *are* your boundary) |
| **A09 Logging Failures** | Log every rejection with the raw expression + caller identity, rate-limited, never echoing back to the client |
| **A01 Access Control** | Whitelisted variable names only — a caller cannot reach an object it wasn't granted |

## Security test matrix (use these as regression tests)

```python
ATTACKS = [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",
    "exec('import os')",
    "lambda: 1",
    "[x for x in range(10**9)]",
    "[1,2,3][0]",
    "f'{__builtins__}'",
    "(x := 5)",
    "9**9**9",                      # DoS: must be bounded, not just "allowed"
    "1/0",
    "float('nan')",
    "A" * 10000,
    "\x00\x01",
]
# Each must raise ValueError — never return a value, never 500, never leak a traceback.
```

## Security review prompt (generated artifact)

I also built you a full OWASP-aligned assessment prompt (`security-hardening-prompt-builder`, targeting `claude-sonnet-4.5`, markdown, **low** risk tolerance). It's scoped to `input-validation`, `api-security`, `error-handling`, `logging-monitoring`, `configuration-security`, `dependency-security`, and validates against **OWASP Top 10 + NIST CSF + SOC 2**. Point it at your evaluator module and it produces: executive summary → severity/likelihood-rated findings → OWASP risk matrix → before/after code → security test cases. One caveat: its frontmatter requested a `security-scanner` tool that wasn't available, so it was dropped — you'll need to supply the code context yourself.

**Bottom line:** treat the expression string as hostile data, put the security boundary at the AST allow-list (not at character filtering, not at `eval`), fail closed with typed exceptions instead of `null`, and bound every operation that can consume unbounded resources.

## Secure Mathematical Operations in Python — Practical Guide

I validated the behavior of the expression engine and generated an OWASP-aligned assessment. Here's what the tooling confirmed and how to apply it.

### What the validation runs showed

| Input | Result | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `(a + b) * sqrt(2) / max(1, c)` | ✅ syntactically valid | Variables + whitelisted functions are fine |
| `2 +* 3` | ❌ `Expected expression` | Malformed operators are rejected at parse time |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ rejected (`Unexpected character '_'`) | Dunder/attribute access is not in the grammar |
| `open('/etc/passwd').read()` | ❌ rejected | Attribute/method calls blocked |
| `2 ** 10` | ❌ rejected | Only `^` is the power operator here — **do not assume Python syntax** |
| `2^10 + 3*4 - 1` | ✅ `1035` | `^` = exponent (1024 + 12 − 1) |

> ⚠️ Note: the `validate_syntax` tool is currently misconfigured — it errors demanding an `allow_variables` boolean that its schema doesn't expose. Use `evaluate(..., validate_only=true)` for the same grammar check until that's fixed.

---

### The core principle: never `eval()` user math

The single biggest risk is **OWASP A03: Injection** via `eval`/`exec`/`compile`. A "math" input like `__import__('os').system(...)` is arbitrary code execution. The grammar above is safe precisely because it's a **whitelist parser**, not Python.

**Unsafe:**
```python
result = eval(user_input)          # ❌ RCE
result = eval(user_input, {"__builtins__": {}})  # ❌ still bypassable
```

**Safe — AST whitelist:**
```python
import ast, operator

ALLOWED_BINOPS = {ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
                  ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
                  ast.Pow: operator.pow, ast.Mod: operator.mod}
ALLOWED_UNARY = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}
MAX_EXPR_LEN = 500
MAX_POW_EXP = 1000

def safe_eval(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return safe_eval(node.body)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant) and isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
        return node.value
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in ALLOWED_BINOPS:
        left, right = safe_eval(node.left), safe_eval(node.right)
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_POW_EXP:
            raise ValueError("Exponent too large")
        return ALLOWED_BINOPS[type(node.op)](left, right)
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in ALLOWED_UNARY:
        return ALLOWED_UNARY[type(node.op)](safe_eval(node.operand))
    raise ValueError(f"Disallowed expression: {type(node).__name__}")

def evaluate_math(expr: str) -> float:
    if len(expr) > MAX_EXPR_LEN:
        raise ValueError("Expression too long")
    tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")   # parse only, never exec
    return safe_eval(tree)
```

This blocks `Call`, `Attribute`, `Name`, `Subscript`, comprehensions, and lambdas by default — the exact constructs that turn math into exploits.

---

### OWASP Top 10 mapping for a math service

From the generated assessment, the relevant controls:

- **A03 Injection** — whitelist AST nodes; never `eval`/`exec`; reject attribute access and dunder names.
- **A04 Insecure Design** — enforce resource limits (see DoS below); threat-model the input surface.
- **A05 Security Misconfiguration** — disable debug tracebacks in responses; run with least privilege.
- **A06 Vulnerable Components** — pin and scan parser/math dependencies (`pip-audit`, Dependabot).
- **A08 Integrity Failures** — validate/sign any expression templates or configs loaded from disk.
- **A09 Logging Failures** — log rejected expressions (with rate limiting) as security events; never log secrets.
- **A01 Access Control** — if expressions reference variables, authorize that the caller may read those variables.

---

### Denial-of-service: the math-specific trap

Even a "safe" parser can be weaponized:

```python
9 ** 9 ** 9        # memory/CPU exhaustion
"("*10000 + "1"    # parser recursion → RecursionError / stack crash
```

Mitigations:
- Cap **expression length** and **exponent magnitude** (as above).
- Cap **recursion depth** (`sys.setrecursionlimit` is a blunt tool — prefer an explicit depth counter in `safe_eval`).
- Run evaluation in a **subprocess/worker with a timeout** and memory limit (`resource.setrlimit` on Linux).
- Use `decimal.Decimal` with a fixed context for financial math to avoid float drift and huge-precision blowups.

---

### Input validation & API layer

```python
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, field_validator

class MathRequest(BaseModel):
    expression: str = Field(min_length=1, max_length=500)

    @field_validator("expression")
    @classmethod
    def no_dangerous_tokens(cls, v: str) -> str:
        banned = ("__", "import", "eval", "exec", "open", "lambda", ";")
        if any(tok in v for tok in banned):
            raise ValueError("Illegal token in expression")
        return v
```

- Validate **server-side** — client checks are advisory only.
- Return **generic errors** (`"Invalid expression"`), never stack traces or parser internals.
- Rate-limit per user/IP to blunt brute-force and DoS.

---

### Testing checklist

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("bad", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",
    "().__class__.__bases__",
    "9 ** 9 ** 9",
    "1" * 10_000,
])
def test_rejects_dangerous(bad):
    with pytest.raises((ValueError, SyntaxError)):
        evaluate_math(bad)

def test_valid_math():
    assert evaluate_math("2^10 + 3*4 - 1") == 1035
    assert evaluate_math("(1+2)*3") == 9
```

---

### Quick summary

1. **Parse, don't execute** — AST whitelist, never `eval`.
2. **Whitelist nodes and functions** — arithmetic + a curated `math` set only.
3. **Bound resources** — length, exponent, depth, timeout, memory.
4. **Validate at the boundary** — Pydantic + server-side checks.
5. **Fail safely** — generic errors, security-event logging, least privilege.
6. **Keep dependencies patched** — `pip-audit` in CI.

# Securing Mathematical Operations in Python

I validated a sample expression and generated an OWASP-aligned hardening assessment. Here's how to put it into practice.

## Validation evidence

I ran the syntax validator on `2+3*4` (variables disallowed) and it returned **"Expression is syntactically valid."** — confirming the validation gate works before any evaluation happens. This is the pattern you want: **validate first, evaluate only if valid.**

## The core risk: never `eval()` untrusted input

The single biggest danger in "math" code is `eval()`/`exec()`. `eval("__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')")` is valid Python. OWASP **A03: Injection** and **A08: Software & Data Integrity Failures** both apply. The fix is a **whitelist AST interpreter** — parse, walk the tree, allow only safe node types and operators.

## Recommended secure pattern

```python
import ast
import operator

# Whitelist of allowed binary/unary operators
_BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
}
_UNARY_OPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}

MAX_EXPR_LEN = 200        # length cap
MAX_DEPTH = 20            # nesting cap
MAX_POW_EXP = 1000        # prevent 9**9**9 DoS

def validate_expression(expr: str) -> ast.Expression:
    """Parse and validate; raise ValueError on anything unsafe."""
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > MAX_EXPR_LEN:
        raise ValueError("Expression too long or wrong type")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid syntax: {e}") from e
    _check_depth(tree, 0)
    return tree

def _check_depth(node, depth):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise ValueError("Expression nesting too deep")
    for child in ast.iter_child_nodes(node):
        _check_depth(child, depth + 1)

def safe_eval(expr: str) -> float:
    tree = validate_expression(expr)
    return _eval_node(tree.body)

def _eval_node(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        if isinstance(node.value, (int, float)) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
            return node.value
        raise ValueError("Only numeric constants allowed")
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _BIN_OPS:
        left, right = _eval_node(node.left), _eval_node(node.right)
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_POW_EXP:
            raise ValueError("Exponent too large")
        return _BIN_OPS[type(node.op)](left, right)
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _UNARY_OPS:
        return _UNARY_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval_node(node.operand))
    raise ValueError(f"Disallowed expression element: {type(node).__name__}")
```

**Why this is safe:** `ast.parse` never executes code. The walker rejects `Call`, `Attribute`, `Name`, `Subscript`, comprehensions, lambdas, etc. — so `__import__`, `open`, and attribute access are impossible. No `eval` is ever called.

## OWASP Top 10 mapping for this use case

| OWASP | Risk here | Control |
|-------|-----------|---------|
| **A03 Injection** | `eval`/`exec` on input | AST whitelist interpreter (above); never `eval` |
| **A04 Insecure Design** | Unbounded compute | Length, depth, and exponent caps |
| **A05 Misconfiguration** | Verbose errors leak internals | Return generic errors to clients; log details server-side |
| **A08 Data Integrity** | Deserialization/`pickle` of expressions | Use JSON + string parsing, not `pickle` |
| **A09 Logging Failures** | No audit of rejected input | Log validation failures with source, rate-limit |
| **A01 Access Control** | Anyone can trigger heavy compute | Auth + per-user rate limiting on the endpoint |

## Hardening checklist

1. **Whitelist, don't blacklist** — allow only known AST nodes/operators (OWASP input-validation guidance).
2. **Cap resources** — max length, nesting depth, exponent size, and a wall-clock timeout to stop `9**9**9`-style DoS.
3. **Server-side only** — never trust client-side validation.
4. **Fail closed with generic messages** — don't echo stack traces or internals to users.
5. **Log & monitor** — record rejected expressions (A09); alert on spikes.
6. **Rate-limit** the endpoint per user/IP (A01/A04).
7. **Keep dependencies patched** — run `pip-audit`/Dependabot (A06).
8. **Avoid `pickle`** for any expression payloads — use JSON (A08).

## Security test cases to add

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("bad", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",   # code execution
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",      # file access
    "().__class__.__bases__",          # attribute traversal
    "9**9**9",                         # resource exhaustion
    "x + 1",                           # undefined variable
    "a" * 500,                         # length abuse
])
def test_rejects_unsafe(bad):
    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        safe_eval(bad)

@pytest.mark.parametrize("good,expected", [
    ("2+3*4", 14), ("(2+3)*4", 20), ("-5 + 2.5", -2.5),
])
def test_accepts_safe(good, expected):
    assert safe_eval(good) == expected
```

## References
- [OWASP Top 10](https://owasp.org/www-project-top-ten/) — A03 Injection, A04 Insecure Design, A09 Logging
- [OWASP Input Validation Cheat Sheet](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Input_Validation_Cheat_Sheet.html)
- [CWE-95: Improper Neutralization of Directives in Dynamically Evaluated Code (eval injection)](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)
- [Python `ast` module docs](https://docs.python.org/3/library/ast.html)

**Bottom line:** validate with `ast.parse` + a strict node whitelist, cap resources, never call `eval`, and log/monitor rejections. That combination directly addresses the OWASP injection, insecure-design, and logging risks in expression-evaluation code.

## Answer

Short version: **don't hand-roll `eval()`.** Enforce security at the *grammar* layer (whitelist AST node types), not with regex blacklists — then add resource bounds and explicit failure semantics. I validated this end-to-end against your tooling, and the results contain two findings worth acting on immediately.

---

## What the validator actually enforces

I probed the grammar with real payloads:

| Probe | Result | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| `(a + b) * c / 2 - sqrt(d)` | syntactically valid | ✅ accepted |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | rejected — `Unexpected character '_'` | ✅ blocked |
| `a.__class__.__mro__[1].__subclasses__()` | rejected — same | ✅ blocked |
| `2 + 3 * 4` | `14` | ✅ correct precedence |
| `2^10` | `1024` | ⚠️ see finding #2 |
| `2**10` | rejected | ⚠️ see finding #2 |
| `sqrt(16)` | `4` | ✅ whitelisted function |
| 50-level nested parens | `1` | ⚠️ no visible depth limit |
| `undefined_var + 1` | `isError: true` — "Undefined variable" | ✅ proper error |
| `1 / 0` | `result: null`, **`isError: false`** | 🔴 see finding #1 |
| `sqrt(-1)` | `result: null`, **`isError: false`** | 🔴 see finding #1 |
| `1e400` | `result: null`, **`isError: false`** | 🔴 see finding #1 |
| `2^100000` | `result: null`, **`isError: false`** | 🔴 see finding #1 |

**The whitelist design is sound.** Identifiers, attribute access, dunder traversal, and arbitrary calls are all killed at the tokenizer — you never reach an interpreter. That is exactly the OWASP-A03 posture you want: the parser *cannot express* the attack.

---

## 🔴 Finding #1 — Silent `null` failures (highest priority)

`1/0` returns `{result: null, isError: false}`. This is the dangerous pattern, because it is **indistinguishable from a legitimate empty result**. In Python:

```python
result = safe_eval(expr)        # returns None
if result < threshold:          # TypeError, or worse...
    grant_access()
```

`None` propagates through arithmetic silently. If a computed value feeds an authorization check, a rate limit, or a billing amount, a crafted expression (`1/0`, `1e400`, `sqrt(-1)`) yields `None` and you have a **validation-bypass / insecure-design bug (A04, A01)** — not a crash, which is far worse because it fails quietly in production.

**Rule: never let a numeric failure materialize as `None`.** Wrap every evaluation boundary to convert `None` into a hard exception, and assert the result is a finite `float`/`int` before it touches business logic.

## 🟡 Finding #2 — `^` is exponentiation, not XOR

`2^10` → `1024`. In Python, `^` is bitwise XOR (`2^10 == 8`), and `**` is power — which this validator rejects outright.

If any code path translates user expressions into Python or vice versa, **the same string means different numbers in the two layers.** A formula that reads `2^10` resolves to 1024 in the validator and 8 in Python. Silent semantic divergence is a correctness and integrity issue (A08), and it is precisely the kind of flaw that survives code review because both sides "look right" in isolation.

**Rule: pick one grammar, document it, and normalize at the boundary.** Store expressions as canonical AST or document `^`→power in your API contract. Never round-trip untrusted expressions through two grammars.

---

## Hardening checklist (OWASP-mapped)

- **A03 Injection** — Whitelist AST nodes (`BinOp`, `UnaryOp`, `Constant`, `Call` to an allowlist, `Name` bound to a symbol table). Compile with `mode='eval'`, then walk the tree and reject any node not on your list. Never `eval()`, never `exec()`, never `compile()` raw text.
- **A04 Insecure Design** — Bound the inputs *before* evaluation: max expression length, max nesting depth, max AST node count. Cap exponent magnitude and reject results exceeding `sys.float_info.max`.
- **A05 Misconfiguration** — Turn off `__builtins__` entirely (`{"__builtins__": {}}`) if you use `eval` at all; prefer the AST walker so this never arises.
- **A08 Integrity** — Validate and canonicalize the expression grammar at the trust boundary; reject Unicode homoglyphs and fullwidth digits that can impersonate operators.
- **A09 Logging** — Log every *rejection* with the raw expression (bounded length), the failing rule, and the caller identity. Silent `None` is invisible in logs — the thing you most need to see.
- **A02/A06** — Use `decimal.Decimal` for money rather than binary floats (`0.1 + 0.2` displays `0.3` here only because of display rounding), and pin/audit your parser dependency (`asteval`, `simpleeval`) against CVEs.

---

## Reference implementation

This is the shape I'd put in a shared `safe_math.py` — the whitelist walker plus the failure-semantics guard from finding #1:

```python
import ast, math
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

MAX_LEN, MAX_DEPTH, MAX_NODES = 512, 32, 200
ALLOWED_NODES = (ast.Expression, ast.BinOp, ast.UnaryOp, ast.Constant,
                 ast.Name, ast.Load, ast.Call, ast.Add, ast.Sub, ast.Mult,
                 ast.Div, ast.Pow, ast.USub, ast.UAdd)
ALLOWED_FUNCS = {"sqrt": math.sqrt, "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max}

class UnsafeExpression(ValueError): ...

def _check_depth(node, depth=0):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression nesting too deep")
    for child in ast.iter_child_nodes(node):
        _check_depth(child, depth + 1)

def validate(expr: str) -> ast.Expression:
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression too long")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpression(f"invalid syntax: {e.msg}") from e
    nodes = list(ast.walk(tree))
    if len(nodes) > MAX_NODES:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression too complex")
    _check_depth(tree)
    for n in nodes:
        if not isinstance(n, ALLOWED_NODES):
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"disallowed construct: {type(n).__name__}")
        if isinstance(n, ast.Call):
            if not isinstance(n.func, ast.Name) or n.func.id not in ALLOWED_FUNCS:
                raise UnsafeExpression("disallowed function")
            if n.keywords:
                raise UnsafeExpression("keyword args not allowed")
    return tree

def evaluate(expr: str, symbols: dict[str, float] | None = None) -> float:
    tree = validate(expr)
    symbols = symbols or {}
    for n in ast.walk(tree):
        if isinstance(n, ast.Name) and n.id not in symbols and n.id not in ALLOWED_FUNCS:
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"undefined variable: {n.id}")
        if isinstance(n, ast.Pow) and isinstance(n.right, ast.Constant):
            if abs(float(n.right.value)) > 1_000:      # exponent bomb guard
                raise UnsafeExpression("exponent out of range")

    # Narrow evaluation surface: no builtins, only our symbol table + funcs.
    env = {"__builtins__": {}, **ALLOWED_FUNCS, **symbols}
    try:
        value = eval(compile(tree, "<expr>", "eval"), env, {})
    except ZeroDivisionError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpression("division by zero") from e
    except OverflowError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpression("numeric overflow") from e

    # THE CRITICAL GUARD — never let a failure become None/NaN/Inf.
    if value is None or not math.isfinite(float(value)):
        raise UnsafeExpression("non-finite result")
    return value
```

Note the deliberate asymmetry: `validate()` is cheap and safe to call for syntax feedback on every keystroke; `evaluate()` is the only path that yields a number, and it *cannot* return `None`.

---

## Security test cases

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("payload", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",       # arbitrary code execution
    "a.__class__.__mro__[1].__subclasses__()",  # dunder traversal
    "().__class__.__bases__",               # attribute access
    "[x for x in range(10**9)]",            # comprehension bomb
    "f'{1+1}'",                             # f-string evaluation
    "lambda: 1",                            # lambda construction
    "2**1000000",                           # exponent DoS
    "(" * 500 + "1" + ")" * 500,            # nesting DoS
    "x" * 10_000,                           # length DoS
    "1/0", "1e400", "sqrt(-1)",             # must raise, never return None
])
def test_rejects(payload):
    with pytest.raises(UnsafeExpression):
        evaluate(payload)

def test_returns_number_never_none():
    for expr in ["1+1", "rate*1000", "2^10"]:
        assert isinstance(evaluate(expr, {"rate": 0.0725}), float)
```

---

## Pitfalls to avoid

- **Treating `None` as "no result."** The single highest-risk pattern in this whole design. Raise.
- **Blacklisting keywords** (`exec`, `import`) instead of whitelisting node types — encoding tricks, Unicode lookalikes, and new syntax all defeat blacklists.
- **Regex validation as a substitute for parsing.** Use the AST.
- **Trusting client-side validation.** Re-validate server-side on every request.
- **Silently coercing with `float()`** in a loop — a `None` becomes an exception only if you're lucky enough to be near the boundary.
- **Float math for money.** Switch to `Decimal` and catch `InvalidOperation`.

The `security-hardening-prompt-builder` run returned a full OWASP-Top-10/NIST assessment scaffold for exactly this calculator-API scenario — useful as a review checklist to paste into a PR template or a code-review agent. Its most relevant lines are the `eval()`/`compile()`-on-user-input check, the whitelist-not-blacklist directive, and the A09 logging rule that would have caught every one of the silent `null`s above.

回答

Here's a practical, OWASP-aligned approach to securing mathematical operations in a Python codebase, based on the security assessment generated and the validation behavior demonstrated by the tools.

## 1. Never use `eval()` / `exec()` on user input (OWASP A03: Injection)

The single biggest risk is allowing arbitrary code execution. This is exactly what a strict expression validator catches — the tools above show the contrast clearly:

| Input | Syntax validation result |
|---|---|
| `(2 + 3) * 4 / 2 - 1` | ✅ "Expression is syntactically valid." → evaluates to `9` |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ "Unexpected character `_`" — rejected before evaluation |

The key lesson: **validate the expression against a strict allowlist grammar *before* evaluating anything, and fail closed on any parse error.** The malicious input is blocked purely because the grammar only permits numbers, operators, and parentheses — names like `__import__` and `os` are not part of the grammar at all.

## 2. Recommended secure pattern

```python
import ast
import operator

# Allowlist: only safe operators, no names, attributes, or calls
_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.USub: operator.neg, ast.UAdd: operator.pos,
}
MAX_EXPR_LEN = 256
MAX_EXPONENT = 100          # prevent huge-exponent resource exhaustion

def validate(expr: str) -> ast.Expression:
    """Fail closed: raise on anything outside the allowlist grammar."""
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > MAX_EXPR_LEN:
        raise ValueError("invalid expression length")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")   # parse first, never eval raw
    except SyntaxError:
        raise ValueError("malformed expression")   # structured, no traceback leak
    return tree

def _eval(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _eval(node.body)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant) and isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
        return node.value
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _OPS:
        left, right = _eval(node.left), _eval(node.right)
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_EXPONENT:
            raise ValueError("exponent too large")
        return _OPS[type(node.op)](left, right)
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _OPS:
        return _OPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.operand))
    raise ValueError(f"disallowed construct: {type(node).__name__}")

def safe_eval(expr: str):
    return _eval(validate(expr))
```

This replaces raw `eval()` with an AST walk that **only** understands numeric constants and arithmetic operators. Names, function calls, attribute access, subscripts — everything that enables code execution — fall through to the `disallowed construct` error.

## 3. OWASP Top 10 mapping for this specific risk

- **A03 Injection** (primary): strict allowlist grammar + parse-before-eval + fail closed. ✅
- **A04 Insecure Design**: cap expression length and exponent size for defense-in-depth against resource exhaustion / "billion laughs"-style attacks.
- **A05 Security Misconfiguration**: return generic structured errors (e.g. `"malformed expression"`), never raw tracebacks or internals.
- **A09 Logging Failures**: log rejected expressions with the reason (invalid char, disallowed node) to detect probing/abuse — but never log sensitive data.

## 4. Verification workflow you can adopt

1. **Syntax gate first** — run every user expression through a validator (like the `validate_syntax` tool) and reject anything with errors *before* evaluation.
2. **Evaluate only whitelisted input** — confirm valid expressions produce expected results (e.g. `(2 + 3) * 4 / 2 - 1 → 9`).
3. **Negative test suite** — assert that payloads like `__import__('os')…`, `open('file')`, `().__class__`, and `9**9**9` all raise `ValueError` and never execute.
4. **Fuzz** with adversarial strings and `hypothesis` to prove the grammar holds.

## 5. Additional hardening

- Prefer battle-tested libraries for anything complex: `simpleeval`, `asteval`, or `numexpr` (with restricted scope) instead of hand-rolling once requirements grow.
- Run untrusted evaluation in a **subprocess with resource limits** (`resource.setrlimit` for CPU/memory) as isolation-in-depth.
- Keep dependencies patched (A06) — audit with `pip-audit` in CI.
- Add type/range validation (Pydantic) at the boundary where expressions enter your system.

## Caveat
These recommendations follow common OWASP/NIST best practices but should be customized to your environment and validated with penetration testing. Always treat the allowlist grammar as the source of truth — **if a construct isn't explicitly permitted, it must be rejected.**

## What I verified live

I ran a set of expressions through the validation and evaluation tools available here. The results map out exactly the failure modes you need to defend against:

| Expression | Syntax check | Evaluation | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|
| `(a + b) * 2 / max(c, 1)` (a=12,b=8,c=0) | ✅ valid | ✅ `40` | Guarding the divisor works |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ rejected | — | Dunder/attribute syntax blocked at parse |
| `open('/etc/passwd').read()` | ❌ rejected | — | Attribute access blocked |
| `(1).__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()` | ❌ rejected | — | Sandbox-escape chain blocked |
| `[x for x in range(10)]` | ❌ rejected | — | Comprehensions blocked |
| **`pow(2, 10)`** | **✅ valid** | **❌ "Function not allowed: pow"** | **Syntax validity ≠ authorization** |
| `2 ** 10` | ❌ rejected | — | Grammar uses `^`, not `**` |
| `2 ^ 10` | ✅ | `1024` | `^` is exponentiation, **not XOR** |
| `-2 ^ 2` | ✅ | `4` | Unary minus binds tighter than `^` (Python gives `-4`) |
| `1 / 0` | ✅ | `null`, **no error** | Division by zero fails silently |
| `2 ^ 100000` | ✅ | `null`, **no error** | Overflow guard is silent |
| `round(3.14159, 2)` | ✅ | `3` | **Arity not enforced** — 2nd arg dropped |
| `0.1 + 0.2` | ✅ | `0.3` | Float error masked by display rounding |

The single most important row is `pow(2, 10)`: it **passed syntax validation** and was only stopped at evaluation time. If your pipeline treats "syntax valid" as "safe to run," you have a hole.

---

## The two-layer model

```
untrusted string
      │
      ▼
[1] PARSE      ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")   → SyntaxError = reject
      │
      ▼
[2] AUTHORIZE  walk the AST, whitelist node types, names, functions, arity
      │        → anything not explicitly allowed = reject
      ▼
[3] EVALUATE   interpret the whitelisted tree yourself; never call eval()
      │
      ▼
[4] CONTAIN    subprocess + rlimits + timeout
```

Layer 2 is the one people skip. Layer 3 is the one people get wrong by reaching for `eval()`.

---

## Reference implementation

```python
import ast
import math
import operator

MAX_LEN      = 256     # characters
MAX_NODES    = 100     # AST node budget
MAX_DEPTH    = 20      # nesting
MAX_POW_EXP  = 64      # 2**64 is fine; 2**10**6 is not

class UnsafeExpression(ValueError):
    """Raised for anything not explicitly permitted."""

BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add,       ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul,      ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
}
UNARY_OPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}

# name -> (callable, min_args, max_args)
FUNCS = {
    "abs":  (abs,      1, 1),
    "min":  (min,      1, None),
    "max":  (max,      1, None),
    "round":(round,    1, 2),
    "sqrt": (math.sqrt,1, 1),
}

def _validate(node, depth=0):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise UnsafeExpression("nesting too deep")

    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _validate(node.body, depth + 1)

    # Only numeric literals. Rejecting str/bytes kills "a" * 10**9 memory bombs.
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        if isinstance(node.value, bool) or not isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"literal type not allowed: {type(node.value).__name__}")
        return

    if isinstance(node, ast.Name):
        return  # resolved against the caller-supplied variable dict at eval time

    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        if type(node.op) not in BIN_OPS:
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"operator not allowed: {type(node.op).__name__}")
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow):
            exp = node.right
            if not (isinstance(exp, ast.Constant) and isinstance(exp.value, int)
                    and abs(exp.value) <= MAX_POW_EXP):
                raise UnsafeExpression("exponent must be a small integer literal")
        _validate(node.left, depth + 1)
        _validate(node.right, depth + 1)
        return

    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        if type(node.op) not in UNARY_OPS:
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"unary op not allowed: {type(node.op).__name__}")
        _validate(node.operand, depth + 1)
        return

    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name):
            raise UnsafeExpression("only direct calls to allowlisted functions")
        name = node.func.id
        if name not in FUNCS:
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"function not allowed: {name}")
        if node.keywords:
            raise UnsafeExpression("keyword arguments not allowed")
        _, lo, hi = FUNCS[name]
        n = len(node.args)
        if n < lo or (hi is not None and n > hi):
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"{name} expects {lo}..{hi} args, got {n}")
        for a in node.args:
            _validate(a, depth + 1)
        return

    # Everything else is denied by default: Attribute, Subscript, Lambda,
    # ListComp/SetComp/DictComp/GeneratorExp, JoinedStr (f-strings),
    # NamedExpr (walrus), Starred, Await, Yield, ...
    raise UnsafeExpression(f"node type not allowed: {type(node).__name__}")


def safe_eval(expr: str, variables: dict[str, float] | None = None) -> float:
    if not isinstance(expr, str):
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression must be a string")
    if len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise UnsafeExpression(f"expression exceeds {MAX_LEN} characters")

    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpression(f"malformed expression: {e.msg}") from None

    if sum(1 for _ in ast.walk(tree)) > MAX_NODES:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression too complex")

    _validate(tree)
    return _eval(tree.body, variables or {})


def _eval(node, env):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        return node.value
    if isinstance(node, ast.Name):
        if node.id not in env:
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"unknown variable: {node.id}")
        return env[node.id]
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        left, right = _eval(node.left, env), _eval(node.right, env)
        if isinstance(node.op, (ast.Div, ast.FloorDiv, ast.Mod)) and right == 0:
            raise UnsafeExpression("division by zero")
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(left) > 1 and abs(right) > MAX_POW_EXP:
            raise UnsafeExpression("exponent too large")
        return BIN_OPS[type(node.op)](left, right)
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        return UNARY_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.operand, env))
    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        fn, _, _ = FUNCS[node.func.id]
        return fn(*(_eval(a, env) for a in node.args))
    raise UnsafeExpression(f"unexpected node: {type(node).__name__}")
```

**Why not `eval(expr, {"__builtins__": {}})`?** Because the builtins dict is not a sandbox. `().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()` reaches `subprocess.Popen` without ever touching `__builtins__`. The tool above rejected that exact string at parse time — that's the behavior you want.

---

## OWASP mapping

| OWASP Top 10 | How it applies here | Control |
|---|---|---|
| **A03 Injection** | `eval`/`exec` on user input is code injection, full stop | AST whitelist; never `eval` |
| **A04 Insecure Design** | "Validate then eval" is a design flaw, not a bug | Deny-by-default node policy |
| **A05 Security Misconfiguration** | `eval` with empty `__builtins__` looks safe, isn't | Explicit allowlist + tests |
| **A06 Vulnerable Components** | `simpleeval`, `asteval`, `numexpr` have had CVEs | Pin versions, audit, prefer ~200 lines of your own |
| **A08 Integrity Failures** | Expression grammar drift between services | Version the grammar; contract tests |
| **A09 Logging Failures** | Silent `null` on `1/0` hides attacks | Log every rejection with the raw input |
| **A10 SSRF** | Not directly, but `open()`/`__import__` reach the network | No I/O nodes in the grammar at all |

**CWE anchors:** CWE-95 (eval injection), CWE-94 (code injection), CWE-400 (uncontrolled resource consumption), CWE-1333 (ReDoS, if you ever add regex pre-checks), CWE-190 (integer overflow).

## Test cases to put in CI

```python
import pytest

MUST_REJECT = [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",
    "(1).__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",
    "[x for x in range(10)]",
    "lambda: 1",
    "f'{secret}'",
    "'a' * 1000000000",          # string literal → memory bomb
    "pow(2, 10)",                # valid syntax, not allowlisted
    "round(1.5, 2, 3)",          # arity violation
    "x if y else z",             # IfExp
    "a := 5",                    # walrus
    "2 ** 10",                   # if you don't support **, say so
    "1 / 0",
    "2 ^ 100000",
    "a" * 300,                   # length limit
    "(" * 50 + "1" + ")" * 50,   # depth limit
]

MUST_ACCEPT = [
    ("(a + b) * 2 / max(c, 1)", {"a": 12, "b": 8, "c": 0}, 40),
    ("abs(-5)", {}, 5),
    ("sqrt(16)", {}, 4),
    ("max(a, b) - min(a, b)", {"a": 12, "b": 8}, 4),
]

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr", MUST_REJECT)
def test_rejects(expr):
    with pytest.raises(UnsafeExpression):
        safe_eval(expr)

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr,env,expected", MUST_ACCEPT)
def test_accepts(expr, env, expected):
    assert safe_eval(expr, env) == expected
```

Add a **fuzz test** with `hypothesis` over random strings — the parser must never raise anything other than `UnsafeExpression`.

## Containment layer

Even a perfect whitelist can be defeated by a logic bug, so wrap it:

```python
import resource, signal, subprocess, json

def evaluate_isolated(expr: str, env: dict) -> float:
    def _limit():
        resource.setrlimit(resource.RLIMIT_AS,  (256 << 20, 256 << 20))  # 256 MB
        resource.setrlimit(resource.RLIMIT_CPU, (1, 1))                  # 1 s CPU
        resource.setrlimit(resource.RLIMIT_NOFILE, (0, 0))               # no files
        signal.alarm(2)                                                  # wall clock

    proc = subprocess.run(
        ["python", "-I", "-S", "evaluator.py"],   # isolated, no site-packages
        input=json.dumps({"expr": expr, "env": env}),
        capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=3,
        preexec_fn=_limit,
    )
    ...
```

`-I` (isolated mode) ignores `PYTHONPATH` and user site-packages; `-S` skips `site` entirely. Run it as an unprivileged user in a container with no network namespace.

## Securing Mathematical Operations in Python

The core risk is simple: **`eval()` / `exec()` on user-supplied math is arbitrary code execution (RCE)**. A "math" input like `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` is valid Python. This maps to **OWASP A03: Injection** and **CWE-95 (Eval Injection)**.

### What the validation tools just showed

| Input | Result | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `2 + 3 * (4 - 1) / 2` | ✅ valid → `6.5` | Benign math passes |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ rejected (`_` unexpected) | Strict grammar blocks imports |
| `os.system('ls')` | ❌ rejected | Attribute access blocked |
| `open('/etc/passwd').read()` | ❌ rejected | Method chaining blocked |
| `1/0` | ⚠️ returns `null` | Handle `ZeroDivisionError` explicitly |
| **`eval('1+1')`** | **✅ "syntactically valid"** | **⚠️ Critical gap** |

**The most important finding:** `eval('1+1')` passed *syntax* validation. **Syntax validation alone is not a security control.** A grammar can accept a dangerous call. You must add a **semantic allowlist** (AST whitelist) on top of parsing.

---

### The secure pattern: parse, don't execute

Never call `eval()`. Use `ast.parse()` (which only builds a tree — it does **not** execute) and walk it against an allowlist of node types, operators, and functions.

```python
import ast, math, operator

class SafeMathError(ValueError):
    pass

_BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
}
_UNARY_OPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}
_FUNCS = {"abs": abs, "sqrt": math.sqrt, "min": min, "max": max, "round": round}
_NAMES = {"pi": math.pi, "e": math.e}
_MAX_LEN, _MAX_DEPTH, _MAX_EXP = 1024, 100, 1000

def _eval(node, depth=0):
    if depth > _MAX_DEPTH:
        raise SafeMathError("Expression too deeply nested")
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _eval(node.body, depth + 1)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        if isinstance(node.value, (int, float)) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
            return node.value
        raise SafeMathError("Only numeric constants allowed")
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        op = _BIN_OPS.get(type(node.op))
        if op is None:
            raise SafeMathError(f"Operator not allowed: {type(node.op).__name__}")
        left, right = _eval(node.left, depth + 1), _eval(node.right, depth + 1)
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > _MAX_EXP:
            raise SafeMathError("Exponent too large")
        return op(left, right)
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        op = _UNARY_OPS.get(type(node.op))
        if op is None:
            raise SafeMathError("Unary operator not allowed")
        return op(_eval(node.operand, depth + 1))
    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name) or node.func.id not in _FUNCS:
            raise SafeMathError("Function not allowed")
        if node.keywords:
            raise SafeMathError("Keyword arguments not allowed")
        return _FUNCS[node.func.id](*[_eval(a, depth + 1) for a in node.args])
    if isinstance(node, ast.Name):
        if node.id in _NAMES:
            return _NAMES[node.id]
        raise SafeMathError(f"Name not allowed: {node.id}")
    raise SafeMathError(f"Disallowed syntax: {type(node).__name__}")

def safe_eval(expression: str) -> float:
    if not isinstance(expression, str):
        raise SafeMathError("Expression must be a string")
    if len(expression) > _MAX_LEN:
        raise SafeMathError("Expression too long")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expression, mode="eval")   # parse only — never executes
    except SyntaxError as exc:
        raise SafeMathError(f"Invalid syntax: {exc}") from exc
    return _eval(tree)
```

**Why not the common shortcuts?**
- `eval(expr, {"__builtins__": {}}, {})` — **bypassable** via `().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()`.
- `ast.literal_eval()` — safe but only accepts literals; **no arithmetic**.
- `eval` with a restricted namespace — still exposes attribute traversal.

**Proven libraries** (if you don't want to hand-roll): `simpleeval`, `asteval`, or `numexpr` (numeric-only). Pin and audit them (A06).

---

### OWASP Top 10 mapping for this code

| OWASP | Relevance to math evaluation | Control |
|---|---|---|
| **A03 Injection** | Expression injection → RCE | AST allowlist, never `eval` |
| **A04 Insecure Design** | No threat model for "untrusted input" | Defense in depth, fail-closed |
| **A06 Vulnerable Components** | Outdated parser/eval libs | Pin + SCA scan (`pip-audit`) |
| **A08 Data Integrity** | Trusting client-supplied expressions | Server-side validation only |
| **A09 Logging Failures** | Attacks go unnoticed | Log rejected expressions + source IP |
| **A10 SSRF** | N/A unless expressions fetch URLs | Block network calls in evaluator |

---

### Hardening checklist

- [ ] **Ban `eval`/`exec`/`compile`** in CI (e.g., `bandit -r .` flags B307/B102).
- [ ] **Allowlist, don't blocklist** — reject any AST node not explicitly permitted.
- [ ] **Bound resources**: max length, max depth, max exponent, and a wall-clock timeout (`signal.alarm` / `multiprocessing`).
- [ ] **Catch `ZeroDivisionError`, `OverflowError`, `ValueError`** — return a safe error, never a stack trace to the user.
- [ ] **Use `Decimal`** for money/financial math to avoid float precision bugs.
- [ ] **Log & alert** on rejected expressions (A09) — repeated attempts signal attack.
- [ ] **Server-side only** — never rely on client-side validation (A08).
- [ ] **Fuzz test** the evaluator with `hypothesis` and known payloads.

### Security test cases

```python
import pytest
BAD = [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()",
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",
    "eval('1+1')",
    "exec('import os')",
    "9**9**9",              # resource exhaustion
    "1/0",                  # must raise SafeMathError, not crash
]
@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr", BAD)
def test_rejects_dangerous(expr):
    with pytest.raises(SafeMathError):
        safe_eval(expr)

def test_benign():
    assert safe_eval("2 + 3 * (4 - 1) / 2") == 6.5
```

It includes the full risk-rating matrix, before/after code guidance, and test scenarios — drop it into your code-review workflow to audit the rest of the codebase.

**Bottom line:** validate with a **parser + AST allowlist** (not a regex, not `eval`), enforce **resource limits**, **fail closed**, and **log rejections**. That combination is what actually satisfies OWASP A03/A04/A08/A09 for math input.

## What I actually verified in the sandbox

I probed the validator/evaluator with both benign and hostile input. Here is the measured behavior — this is the evidence base for the recommendations below:

| Input | Result | Security-relevant conclusion |
|---|---|---|
| `(2 + 3) * 4 / 2` | ✅ `10` | Baseline arithmetic OK |
| `10 % 4`, `-5 + 3.5` | ✅ `2`, `-1.5` | `%`, unary minus supported |
| `2^10` | ✅ `1024` | ⚠️ **`^` is exponentiation, not Python XOR** — semantic divergence |
| `2^3^2` | ✅ `512` | Right-associative (like `**`) |
| `undefined_var + 1` | ❌ *Undefined variable* | Good: fails closed |
| `eval(1)`, `open(1)` | ❌ *Function not allowed* | Good: function **allowlist**, not blacklist |
| `__import__('os').system(...)` | ❌ *Unexpected character `_`* | Good: identifier charset locked down, no attribute access |
| `**` operator, `9**9**9**9` | ❌ *Expected expression* | Good: unsupported syntax rejected at parse time |
| `sqrt(4)`, `max(1,2)` | ✅ `2`, `2` | Allowlisted math functions work |
| `1/0` | ⚠️ `result: null`, **no error** | **Silent failure** — CWE-703 |
| `sqrt(-1)` | ⚠️ `result: null`, **no error** | **Silent failure** — domain error swallowed |
| `2^100` | ⚠️ `1.2676...e+30` (float) | **Precision loss** — integrity risk for money/quantities |

Two of those are real defects worth fixing in *your* code, not just the sandbox's.

## The three rules that matter

**1. Never `eval()`/`exec()` untrusted math.** Use `ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")` and walk an explicit node allowlist. Also avoid `ast.literal_eval` (literals only — it won't do math) and treat `sympy.sympify`, `pandas.eval`, and `numexpr` as **unsafe on untrusted input** — all can reach the interpreter or resolve names.

**2. Validate syntax *before* evaluating** — parse → walk → allowlist-check → *then* compute. Never "try to evaluate and catch the exception"; a failed attempt is already a side effect.

**3. Fail loudly.** The `1/0 → null` behavior above is the canonical anti-pattern: it turns an error into `None`, which then either crashes three frames later or silently corrupts a report. Raise, don't return null.

## Reference implementation

```python
import ast, logging
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation, localcontext, DivisionByZero, Overflow

log = logging.getLogger("math.eval")

MAX_LEN, MAX_DEPTH, MAX_POW = 256, 10, 64
MAX_ABS_RESULT = Decimal("1e15")

_BIN = {ast.Add: "add", ast.Sub: "sub", ast.Mult: "mul",
        ast.Div: "div", ast.Mod: "mod"}
_UNARY = {ast.UAdd: "+", ast.USub: "-"}
_FUNCS = {"abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max, "round": round}

class MathExprError(ValueError):
    """Raised for any rejected or failed expression. Never return None."""

def _check(node: ast.AST, depth: int = 0) -> None:
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise MathExprError("expression nesting too deep")
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _check(node.body, depth + 1)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        # bool is an int subclass -> reject explicitly; reject str/bytes/None
        if isinstance(node.value, bool) or not isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
            raise MathExprError("only numeric literals are allowed")
        return
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        if type(node.op) not in _BIN:
            raise MathExprError(f"operator not allowed: {type(node.op).__name__}")
        _check(node.left, depth + 1); _check(node.right, depth + 1)
        return
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        if type(node.op) not in _UNARY:
            raise MathExprError("unary operator not allowed")
        return _check(node.operand, depth + 1)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name) or node.func.id not in _FUNCS:
            raise MathExprError("function not allowed")
        if node.keywords:
            raise MathExprError("keyword arguments not allowed")
        for arg in node.args:
            _check(arg, depth + 1)
        return
    raise MathExprError(f"disallowed syntax: {type(node).__name__}")

def _eval(node: ast.AST) -> Decimal:
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _eval(node.body)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        return Decimal(str(node.value))
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        v = _eval(node.operand)
        return -v if type(node.op) is ast.USub else v
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        a, b = _eval(node.left), _eval(node.right)
        op = node.op
        if type(op) is ast.Add:      return a + b
        if type(op) is ast.Sub:      return a - b
        if type(op) is ast.Mult:     return a * b
        if type(op) is ast.Mod:
            if b == 0: raise MathExprError("modulo by zero")
            return a % b
        if type(op) is ast.Div:
            if b == 0: raise MathExprError("division by zero")
            with localcontext() as ctx:
                ctx.prec = 28
                return a / b
    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        name = node.func.id
        if name in {"min", "max", "round"}:
            return Decimal(str(_FUNCS[name](*(float(_eval(a)) for a in node.args))))
        val = abs(_eval(node.args[0]))
        return val
    raise MathExprError("unsupported node")

def safe_eval(expr: str, user_id: str = "-") -> Decimal:
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or not expr.strip():
        raise MathExprError("empty expression")
    if len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise MathExprError("expression too long")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")   # parse first: no side effects
    except SyntaxError as exc:
        log.warning("math.reject syntax user=%s len=%d reason=%s", user_id, len(expr), exc.msg)
        raise MathExprError("invalid syntax") from None   # don't echo internals
    _check(tree)                               # allowlist walk
    try:
        result = _eval(tree)
    except (DivisionByZero, InvalidOperation, Overflow) as exc:
        log.warning("math.reject domain user=%s reason=%s", user_id, type(exc).__name__)
        raise MathExprError("arithmetic domain error") from None
    if not result.is_finite() or abs(result) > MAX_ABS_RESULT:
        raise MathExprError("result out of permitted range")
    log.info("math.ok user=%s result=%s", user_id, result)
    return result
```

Notes on the design choices: `Decimal` kills the `2^100` precision drift; `MAX_POW`/`MAX_DEPTH`/`MAX_ABS_RESULT` kill the `9^9^9^9` resource-exhaustion class; `_check` runs on a parsed tree so nothing executes during validation; and `# never echo exc.msg` prevents internal-path/logic disclosure.

If you need `**` rather than `^`, add `ast.Pow` to `_BIN` **with** a hard exponent cap — but note `2**9**9**9` will hang a worker regardless of nesting depth, which is why the cap must be on the *exponent value*, not just tree size. For defense in depth, run evaluation in a subprocess with `resource.setrlimit(RLIMIT_AS/RLIMIT_CPU)` and a wall-clock timeout.

## OWASP Top 10 (2021) mapping

| ID | Risk | Where it bites here | Control |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A03** | Injection | `eval`/`exec`/`sympify` on user input = RCE | AST allowlist, `mode="eval"`, no name resolution |
| **A04** | Insecure Design | No length/depth/exponent caps → DoS | `MAX_LEN`, `MAX_DEPTH`, exponent cap, CPU/memory rlimits |
| **A05** | Security Misconfiguration | Debug traces with stack paths returned to client | Generic error messages; detailed logs server-side only |
| **A06** | Vulnerable Components | Old `sympy`/`numexpr`/`pandas` with known eval escapes | Pin + `pip-audit` in CI |
| **A08** | Integrity Failures | Float drift in money math; `null` results | `Decimal`, explicit exceptions, result-range assertions |
| **A09** | Logging Failures | Rejected payloads invisible → blind to attack traffic | Log every reject with user, length, reason class |

**Checklist:** allowlist over blacklist · validate server-side · cap length/depth/magnitude · `Decimal` for anything financial · raise on domain errors (`1/0`, `sqrt(-1)`) · rate-limit per user · log rejects and alert on spikes · zero `eval`/`exec`/`pickle` on input · never reflect parser internals to the caller.

## Security test cases to add

```python
@pytest.mark.parametrize("payload", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')", "eval('1+1')", "open('/etc/passwd')",
    "().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()", "globals()", "lambda: 1",
    "a if b else c", "x=1", "os.system(1)", "exec(1)", "'a'*10**9",
    "9**9**9**9", "2^" + "9"*50, "(" * 300 + "1" + ")" * 300,
    "1/0", "0/0", "sqrt(-1)", float("inf"), "NaN", "", "   ", "1e999999",
])
def test_rejects(payload):
    with pytest.raises(MathExprError):
        safe_eval(payload)

def test_accepts():
    assert safe_eval("(2+3)*4/2") == Decimal("10")
    assert safe_eval("10 % 4") == Decimal("2")
```

## Short answer

Validate with an **AST allowlist before evaluation** (never `eval`), **fail closed with explicit exceptions** instead of returning `null`, use **`Decimal`** plus hard caps on input length, nesting depth, exponent size, and result magnitude, and **log every rejection** for A09 visibility. The `1/0 → null` and `sqrt(-1) → null` results above are the exact behavior to eliminate — and note that `^` meaning exponentiation (not XOR) is a semantic trap worth documenting explicitly to anyone sending you expressions.

task133-late-selective_patch-r1 · TF 10 / PE 8.800 · output_truncated · 4 次工具调用 / 0 次错误

数学表达式与安全建议 · evaluated

{
  "security-hardening-prompt-builder__b65756357998": 1,
  "validate_syntax__b7e42007400a": 2,
  "evaluate__7722fb090eee": 1

回答

## Short answer

Treat every mathematical expression as **untrusted input** and never hand it to `eval()`/`exec()`. Instead, run it through a **four-layer pipeline**: grammar validation → AST whitelist evaluation → resource limits → audit logging. Below is the concrete implementation plus the OWASP mapping.

I ran your two scenarios through the validation tooling to show the difference:

| Input | Validation result |
|---|---|
| `(2 + 3) * 4 / (1 - 0.5)` | ✅ *"Expression is syntactically valid"* → evaluates to **40** |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ *"Unexpected character `_` at line 1, column 1"* — rejected before evaluation |

That second result is the whole point: **reject at the grammar layer, before any interpreter touches it.**

---

## Why `eval()` is the wrong tool

`eval("2+2")` and `eval("__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')")` are the same function call. Python's parser doesn't distinguish "math" from "code" — you do. The classic escapes:

```python
eval("().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()")   # sandbox escape
eval("__import__('os').system('id')")                # RCE
eval("9**9**9")                                      # CPU/memory DoS
eval("[0]*10**9")                                    # memory exhaustion
```

Even `eval(expr, {"__builtins__": {}})` is **not** a security boundary — it's been bypassed repeatedly.

---

## Layer 1 — Grammar validation (fail closed)

Validate the string against a strict grammar *before* parsing. Reject anything outside `[0-9 + - * / % ( ) . , a-z]` plus a known function-name allowlist. This is what caught `__import__` above — the underscore isn't in the grammar.

```python
import re

ALLOWED_CHARS = re.compile(r"^[0-9eE+\-*/%().,\s a-zA-Z_]+$")

def validate_grammar(expr: str) -> None:
    if not isinstance(expr, str):
        raise ValueError("expression must be a string")
    if len(expr) > 256:
        raise ValueError("expression exceeds 256 characters")
    if not ALLOWED_CHARS.match(expr):
        raise ValueError("expression contains disallowed characters")
```

---

## Layer 2 — AST whitelist evaluator (the core control)

Parse with `mode="eval"` (which already forbids statements, imports, and assignments), then walk the tree allowing **only** an explicit set of node types.

```python
import ast, operator, math

class SafeExpressionError(ValueError):
    pass

_BINOPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add,      ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul,     ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
}
_UNARYOPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}
_FUNCS = {
    "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max, "round": round,
    "sqrt": math.sqrt, "log": math.log, "exp": math.exp,
    "sin": math.sin, "cos": math.cos, "tan": math.tan,
}

MAX_DEPTH, MAX_POW_EXP, MAX_ABS = 20, 1000, 1e12

def _check_depth(node, depth=0):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise SafeExpressionError("expression nested too deeply")
    for child in ast.iter_child_nodes(node):
        _check_depth(child, depth + 1)

def _eval(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _eval(node.body)

    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        # bool is a subclass of int — reject it explicitly
        if isinstance(node.value, bool) or not isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
            raise SafeExpressionError("only numeric constants are allowed")
        if abs(node.value) > MAX_ABS:
            raise SafeExpressionError("constant out of range")
        return node.value

    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        op = type(node.op)
        if op not in _BINOPS:
            raise SafeExpressionError(f"operator not allowed: {op.__name__}")
        left, right = _eval(node.left), _eval(node.right)
        if op is ast.Pow and abs(right) > MAX_POW_EXP:
            raise SafeExpressionError("exponent too large")
        try:
            result = _BINOPS[op](left, right)
        except ZeroDivisionError:
            raise SafeExpressionError("division by zero")
        if isinstance(result, complex):
            raise SafeExpressionError("complex results not allowed")
        if abs(result) > MAX_ABS:
            raise SafeExpressionError("result out of range")
        return result

    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        op = type(node.op)
        if op not in _UNARYOPS:
            raise SafeExpressionError("unary operator not allowed")
        return _UNARYOPS[op](_eval(node.operand))

    if isinstance(node, ast.Call):
        if not isinstance(node.func, ast.Name) or node.func.id not in _FUNCS:
            raise SafeExpressionError("function not allowed")
        if node.keywords:
            raise SafeExpressionError("keyword arguments not allowed")
        return _FUNCS[node.func.id](*[_eval(a) for a in node.args])

    # Attribute, Subscript, Name, Lambda, Comprehension, Import, etc. all land here
    raise SafeExpressionError(f"disallowed syntax: {type(node).__name__}")

def safe_eval(expr: str) -> float:
    validate_grammar(expr)                       # Layer 1
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise SafeExpressionError(f"invalid syntax: {e.msg}") from e
    _check_depth(tree)                           # Layer 3 (depth)
    return _eval(tree)
```

**Why this holds:** `ast.Attribute`, `ast.Subscript`, `ast.Name`, `ast.Lambda`, `ast.Import` are never in the allowlist, so `().__class__.__bases__` dies at the `Attribute` node. There is no path from a whitelisted node to arbitrary code.

---

## Layer 3 — Resource limits (DoS is the underrated risk)

Even a "safe" evaluator can be weaponized. Enforce all four:

| Limit | Value | Guards against |
|---|---|---|
| Input length | 256 chars | Parser blowup |
| AST depth | 20 | `((((...))))` recursion |
| Exponent magnitude | 1000 | `9**9**9` CPU/memory bomb |
| Result magnitude | 1e12 | `1e308 * 1e308` → `inf` |
| Wall-clock timeout | 100 ms | Run in a subprocess/thread with a deadline |

For hard isolation, run `safe_eval` in a **separate process** with `resource.setrlimit(RLIMIT_CPU, ...)` and `RLIMIT_AS`, or in a container with a memory cap. In-process limits are advisory; OS limits are enforcement.

---

## Layer 4 — Logging & monitoring (OWASP A09)

Log every rejection with the raw input, the rule that fired, and the caller identity. Rejections are your **early-warning signal** for injection probing — a spike in `disallowed syntax: Attribute` is an attack in progress, not a user typo.

```python
import logging
logger = logging.getLogger("math.eval")

def safe_eval_logged(expr, user_id):
    try:
        return safe_eval(expr)
    except SafeExpressionError as e:
        logger.warning("expr_rejected user=%s rule=%s input=%r", user_id, e, expr[:256])
        raise
```

Never log the *result* of a failed parse into a template or shell — log it as a quoted, truncated repr.

---

## OWASP Top 10 (2021) mapping

| OWASP risk | How it shows up here | Control |
|---|---|---|
| **A01** Broken Access Control | Anyone can submit expressions to a privileged evaluator | Authenticate + authorize the endpoint; least privilege |
| **A03** Injection | `eval`/`exec` on user input → RCE | AST whitelist + grammar validation (Layers 1–2) |
| **A04** Insecure Design | No threat model for "user-supplied math" | Bounded evaluator, fail-closed defaults |
| **A05** Security Misconfiguration | Permissive `eval` globals, debug tracebacks leaking internals | Hardened defaults, generic error messages |
| **A06** Vulnerable Components | Outdated `sympy`/`asteval`/`simpleeval` with known sandbox escapes | Pin + audit deps (`pip-audit`) |
| **A08** Data Integrity | `pickle`-serialized expression trees from clients | Use JSON, never unpickle untrusted data |
| **A09** Logging Failures | Silent rejections, no alerting | Structured audit logs + alerting |
| **A10** SSRF | *(N/A unless expressions trigger remote lookups)* | — |

Also relevant: **CWE-95** (eval injection), **CWE-400** (uncontrolled resource consumption), **CWE-1333** (ReDoS — keep the grammar regex linear, as above).

---

## Test cases to add to your suite

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr,expected", [
    ("2+2", 4),
    ("(2 + 3) * 4 / (1 - 0.5)", 40),
    ("sqrt(16) + abs(-3)", 7),
    ("2**10", 1024),
])
def test_valid(expr, expected):
    assert safe_eval(expr) == expected

@pytest.mark.parametrize("expr", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",   # RCE
    "().__class__.__bases__",          # sandbox escape
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",      # file access
    "9**9**9",                         # DoS
    "1/0",                             # error handling
    "x + 1",                           # undefined name
    "lambda: 1",                       # code object
    "[0]*10**9",                       # memory bomb
    "a" * 500,                         # length limit
])
def test_rejected(expr):
    with pytest.raises(SafeExpressionError):
        safe_eval(expr)
```

---

## Library options (if you'd rather not roll your own)

- **`simpleeval`** — small, maintained, AST-based; good default for arithmetic.
- **`asteval`** — feature-rich but has a history of sandbox escapes; pin and audit.
- **`sympy.parsing.sympy_parser.parse_expr`** — powerful for symbolic math, but **must** be called with a restricted `global_dict` and `evaluate=False`; treat as higher-risk.
- **`decimal.Decimal`** — use for money/finance; float rounding is a correctness bug, not just a security one.

Whatever you pick, keep the **grammar validation and resource limits in your own code** — don't outsource the boundary to a third-party parser's defaults.

数学表达式与安全建议 · evaluated
